- [ ] **Cross-service error-metric strategy is incoherent — rationalize**: The `record_error(operation, error_type, status_code)` → `*_errors_total{operation,error_type,status_code}` global error counter pattern is wired inconsistently across services with no documented policy:
  - **AC** (30+ call sites, `crates/ac-service/src/handlers/`): wired at every handler error branch. Uses a structured `ErrorCategory` enum (authentication / authorization / cryptographic / internal) derived `From<&AcError>`. The global counter is AC's *only* error visibility — no scoped `*_failures_total` counters exist.
  - **GC** (6 call sites, `crates/gc-service/src/services/{ac,mc}_client.rs`): wired narrowly at downstream-dependency call boundaries. 3 operations (`ac_meeting_token`, `ac_guest_token`, `mc_grpc`). String-literal error_type values, not enum-backed.
  - **MC** (0 call sites at HEAD, ADR-0032 Step 3 follow-up: removing): scoped per-subsystem `*_failures_total` counters cover the operationally interesting surface with richer labels. Global counter is strictly redundant.
  - **MH** (0 call sites at HEAD): same shape as MC; same disposition expected.

  The result: cross-service alerting cannot rely on a uniform `sum by (service) (rate(*_errors_total[5m]))` query because the semantic meaning of "error" differs (AC: any handler failure with structured category; GC: only downstream-dependency failures; MC/MH: deliberately empty). Decide and document one of: (a) every service must have a global `*_errors_total` wired at the error boundary, with scoped counters as additional fidelity (uniform but adds redundancy in MC/MH); (b) global `*_errors_total` is for services without scoped failure counters (current AC pattern), scoped-only is for services with rich subsystem decomposition (current MC/MH direction) — document the choice criteria in ADR-0011; (c) drop the global pattern entirely, standardize on scoped `*_failures_total{subsystem,error_type}` (consistent fidelity, larger migration). Preferred option likely (b) given existing investment, but the criteria need writing down. Owner: observability + code-reviewer; output: ADR-0011 amendment or new ADR. Surfaced during ADR-0032 Step 3 follow-up.

- [ ] **MC notification caller-attribution gap (story 2026-04-12-mh-quic-connection task 17 / R-35 follow-up)**: `mc-incident-response.md` Scenario 13's authenticated-misbehavior branch needs to attribute unexpected `mc_mh_notifications_received_total` events to a specific calling MH instance. Today's signal stack is weaker than the runbook implies, in three layers:

    1. **Metric label gap**: `mc_mh_notifications_received_total` has only `event_type ∈ {connected, disconnected}` — no `source_id` / `source_handler_id` / `client_id` label. Adding one requires bounded values (allowlist clamp at the recording site, same shape as the GC `caller_type_rejected_total{actual_type}` clamp tracked elsewhere in this file) to avoid unbounded cardinality from forged or off-spec tokens.

    2. **Auth interceptor success-path log is TRACE, not DEBUG**: `crates/mc-service/src/grpc/auth_interceptor.rs:259-263` emits `service_type` at `tracing::trace!` on the success path. Production with default `RUST_LOG=info` does not see it. The runbook (MC Sc 13 step 5) currently states "auth interceptor logs the calling service identity at debug level on each request" — that's incorrect; bump the level (or fix the wording).

    3. **`service_type` is non-discriminating across MH instances**: even with TRACE enabled, `service_type` is `"media-handler"` for any MH pod. The cryptographically-attested discriminating field is the JWT's `client_id` (or `sub`), which the success-path log does not include today. The rejection path (line 239-244) logs `service_type` at WARN — but unexpected-notification scenarios by definition pass auth; that log line never fires for this case.

    4. **Self-reported `handler_id` in the request body is the only attribution available today**, and it's exactly what MC Sc 11/13 already frames as untrusted client input — the rogue branch can't trust the rogue caller's self-report.

    Suggested resolution (single observability devloop covering both ends):
    (a) Bump auth interceptor success-path log to INFO and include `client_id` (and `service_type`) under `target = "mc.grpc.auth"` so per-call caller identity is visible in production logs. Rejection path already at WARN; no change there.
    (b) Add a bounded `source_id` label to `mc_mh_notifications_received_total` populated from validated `client_id` claims (NOT from request-body `handler_id`), with allowlist clamping at the recording site mirroring the GC `caller_type_rejected_total{actual_type}` cardinality-clamping pattern.
    (c) Update MC Sc 13 step 5 wording to either match (a)+(b) or, if the implementation is deferred, soften to reflect that today's attribution is via correlated logs + active session inventory, not via independent cryptographic caller identity.

    Owner: observability + meeting-controller (auth interceptor edits) + operations (runbook wording update). Scope: ~10 LoC in auth_interceptor.rs + ~5 LoC at the metrics recording site + bounded-set entry in `docs/observability/metrics/mc-service.md` + 1-paragraph runbook update. Surfaced 2026-05-01 by operations during devloop `2026-05-01-mh-quic-runbooks` and confirmed 2026-05-01 by user review of the devloop's tech-debt log.

- [ ] **Missing alert: MC RegisterMeeting Coordination Failures (story 2026-04-12-mh-quic-connection task 17 / R-34/R-35 follow-up)**: `mc-incident-response.md` Scenario 12 (added 2026-05-01) documents metric-driven triage on `mc_register_meeting_total{status="error"}` rate and `mc_register_meeting_duration_seconds` p95 — both emit in production but no Prometheus alert pages on degradation. This is an omission in the user story (R-34/R-35 scoped runbooks only; alerting was not called out as task 17 work even though task 13 created `MCMediaConnectionAllFailed`) or in the MC implementation (task 8, which added the async RegisterMeeting trigger with retry+backoff but no alert rule). Runbook leaves a fully-formed "Alert candidate" breadcrumb at `mc-incident-response.md` Sc 12 with a candidate threshold (error-rate ratio `> 0.10` for 5m, mirroring `MCHighJoinFailureRate` shape at `infra/docker/prometheus/rules/mc-alerts.yaml:121-130`) plus the matching `runbook_url` (`#scenario-12-registermeeting-coordination-failures`). Add `MCRegisterMeetingFailureRate` (warning) and consider also `MCRegisterMeetingLatencyHigh` (info, p95 > 1s for 5m, mirrors `MCHighJoinLatency`) once ground-truth latency baselines are observed in production. Owner: observability. Scope: ~15 LoC in `mc-alerts.yaml` + alert-rules guard re-run. Surfaced 2026-05-01 by operations during devloop `2026-05-01-mh-quic-runbooks`. **ESCALATED 2026-09-01 by @operations at Gate 3 of `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-09-01-internal-contract-reshape/` — this stopped being a nice-to-have and acquired a blocker.** (i) **It is now an ADR-mandated control, not a gap.** ADR-0036 §8 makes `RegisterMeeting` *the* MC→MH control plane and states under Supporting requirements: "**Re-assert failures are counted and paged.** A silently failing cadence is the same blackhole with extra steps, and nothing else detects it." The counted half shipped long ago and is dashboarded in four places (`infra/grafana/dashboards/mc-overview.json:3097,3213,3224,3235`; `mh-overview.json:1556,2047`); the paged half is this entry. A green dashboard panel is a convincing-looking absence of an alert, which is why this survived four months. Grep confirms **zero** references to `mc_register_meeting_total` or `mh_register_meeting_timeouts_total` across all five files in `infra/docker/prometheus/rules/`. (ii) **There is a hard blocker and naming it is the point of this update.** ~~`docs/observability/slos.md` **does not exist**~~ — **UPDATED 2026-09-02 (story task 7): the file now EXISTS and is authoritative for SLO targets per ADR-0011:40. It does NOT clear this blocker.** Task 7 defined the MH forwarding SLI and its measurement point (ingress-read-complete → egress-enqueued) and recorded the **target as OPEN, ratified in story 8**. The blocker was never the missing file — it was the absence of a ratified target, and that is still absent. What changed is that the wall is now legible instead of invisible: `slos.md` states the rule (no MH burn-rate alert may be authored until the target is ratified) in one authoritative place, and `mh-alerts.yaml`'s deliberate-omission comments were corrected to point at the target rather than the file. The stale "(to be created)" hedges at `docs/observability/alerts.md:929` and `gc-incident-response.md:2035` were retired; `ac-service-incident-response.md:1074` already pointed cleanly. `infra/docker/prometheus/rules/mh-alerts.yaml:11-15` already refuses to write a burn-rate pair for exactly this reason — "cargo-culting a 10x/5x burn-rate pair without an authoritative SLO target would produce a threshold with no principled justification" — and that reasoning applies unchanged here. Anyone picking this up without `slos.md` hits the same wall and either invents a number or defers a third time. **the FILE `slos.md` lands at story task 7** (the file, not the target — story 8 ratifies the MH number, and story 8 is a future user story with no file in `docs/user-stories/` yet) (`docs/user-stories/2026-08-27-hear-yourself-through-handler.md:195`, owner observability; required by ADR-0036's amendment table at line 1272), so the *file* dependency clears inside this story. **The blocker does not** — that is the ratified target, and it is out-of-story by user decision (`docs/user-stories/2026-08-27-hear-yourself-through-handler.md:84`, "Performance → story 8"). ~~Two-stage: **task 7** defines the re-assert-failure objective and its measurement point; **the handler-restart story** lands the rule against that threshold~~ — **CORRECTED 2026-09-02: stage 1 was NOT discharged and has moved.** Task 7 created `slos.md` and defined the MH *forwarding* SLO's measurement point, but it did **not** define a re-assert-failure objective, and `slos.md`'s own Open items table records "No SLO for the MC→MH control plane's applied-generation echo" as still open. The reason is structural rather than an oversight: there is no re-assert cadence and no applied-generation emission in the tree yet (they land with the handler-restart story), so an objective defined now would have no metric to attach to and no observed behaviour to calibrate against — the same "threshold with no principled justification" trap this entry already names. **Revised plan: both stages land in the handler-restart story** — the objective and its measurement point (ADR-0036 §10 Tier 1b: assert on the *acknowledged generation in the response*, never MC internals, so it survives refactoring) first, then the rule and the §11 "sustained re-assert failure" runbook scenario against it, alongside MC's cadence emission. Anyone picking this up before that story still hits the wall; what changed is that the wall is now named and legible instead of being discovered. (iii) **Ownership is not what this entry's original "Owner: observability" says.** Per ADR-0031:36-45 the per-service artifact `infra/docker/prometheus/rules/mc-alerts.yaml` is **meeting-controller**'s, with observability (threshold plausibility, SLO linkage) and operations (severity routing, runbook linkage) as mandatory cross-cutting *reviewers*; ADR-0011:40 makes `slos.md` itself jointly **observability + operations**. That joint ownership is a second reason this gap persisted — it is nobody's sole property, so both parties can assume the other holds it. Stated explicitly so the next reader cannot make that assumption. (iv) **ADR-0031:56 review-graph rule applies**: the generation-divergence metric observes MC↔MH coordination by construction — MC emits it, but its *value* is a statement about MH's apply path — so **media-handler is a required reviewer alongside meeting-controller**, not just the emitting side. Naming it here saves a round trip discovered at review time. (v) **Process note, recorded because it is the actual lesson**: @operations and @observability spent several Gate-1 exchanges designing a new entry for this before either searched `docs/TODO.md` and found it had been filed four months earlier. CLAUDE.md's "confirm something isn't already the SSoT before duplicating it" applies to this file as much as to code, and a `docs/TODO.md` grep belongs at the *start* of a debt-filing discussion, not the end.

- [ ] **Migrate ADR-0032-era metric-test files from `#![allow]` to `#![expect]` per ADR-0002 §expect-over-allow (cross-step batched cleanup)**: All 13 AC Step 4 integration tests use `#![allow(clippy::unwrap_used, clippy::expect_used)]` instead of the ADR-0002-preferred `#![expect(...)]` form. The drift originated in MC Step 3 (`crates/mc-service/tests/actor_metrics_integration.rs`, `redis_metrics_integration.rs`, etc.) and was matched by AC Step 4 for consistency with the established neighbor pattern. Affected scope spans MH Step 2 + MC Step 3 + AC Step 4 metric-test files. The lints are fulfilled in every file (each actively uses `.unwrap()`/`.expect()`), so the migration is a stylistic improvement — switching all files to `#![expect(clippy::unwrap_used, clippy::expect_used, reason = "test-only assertion helpers; unwrap surfaces test failures with clearer panics")]` will compile cleanly without unfulfilled-lint footguns. Cross-step scope makes this a single batched edit (fewer churn-risk regressions than per-step amendments). Owner: observability + code-reviewer. Surfaced during ADR-0032 Step 4 @code-reviewer CR-7 nit (non-blocking, deferred per reviewer recommendation).

- [ ] **`docs/runbooks/devloop-validation.md` §4 "ERROR-only-emitted-by-`_get_base_ref.sh`" claim is pre-existing inaccurate** — §4 of the devloop-validation runbook states that the `ERROR:` token is "emitted only by `scripts/lang/_get_base_ref.sh`". A quick grep shows 7+ other simple guards also emit `ERROR:` (e.g., `validate-alert-rules.sh::run_python_validator` error paths). The claim is pre-existing from task #39's initial runbook landing and was not surfaced or fixed in this devloop. - [ ] **Alert aggregation conventions: instance / region / global**: `alert-conventions.md` doesn't currently document **when to use per-instance, per-region, or fleet-wide aggregation** in alert expressions. Different signal classes call for different aggregation levels, and the missing convention is producing real user-facing blind spots. **Motivating example — `MCGCHeartbeatWarning`**: uses unqualified `sum(rate(...)) / sum(rate(...))` across the fleet. Catches acute fleet-wide failure. Silently misses sustained regional partial degradation: if all MCs in a region persistently run at ~7% heartbeat error rate, the fleet aggregate hovers around ~5% and never crosses the 10% threshold. User-visible failures in that region accumulate without the alert firing. (Pre-consolidation two-tier alert had the same shape; FU#2's consolidation didn't introduce the gap, it just made the absence of regional coverage more visible.) **The general problem**: alert expressions implicitly choose an aggregation level. Each level catches a different failure class — instance-level (`by (pod)`/`by (replica)`) for single-instance crashes/deploys; regional (`by (region)`/`by (zone)`/`by (az)`) for shared-infra issues that present as "everyone in one place is equally degraded" (**today absent from every Dark Tower service's alert set**); fleet-wide (unqualified or `sum by (service)`) for service-wide failure where healthy instances can't cover (current default in all warnings/pages). Related: observability reviewer's FU#2 forward-looking note about `MCGCHeartbeatWarning` severity being load-bearing on ≥ 2-replica topology — same shape (fleet cardinality is an unstated assumption); service specialists authoring their first alerts under ADR-0031 don't currently have guidance on which level to pick. **Desired output**: (1) new §"Aggregation-level conventions" in `docs/observability/alert-conventions.md`, with anchor examples per level including when to pair multiple (e.g., fleet `warning` + regional `warning` covering different failure modes); (2) audit existing `<svc>-alerts.yaml` files for each service, flagging alerts whose current aggregation leaves a gap (likely produces a batch of `MCGCHeartbeatRegional`-style additions); (3) consider a `[reviewer-only]` guidance rule in the conventions doc's rule index making aggregation-level-choice a review prompt when authoring new alerts. **Debate-worthy** — probably wants `/debate "alert aggregation conventions"` with observability + operations + all service specialists before drafting. Owner: observability (conventions owner) + meeting-controller (surfaced the motivating case) + all service specialists at debate time. No deadline. Surfaced 2026-04-18 (commit `de3a275`, originally in misplaced `/work/TODO.md`; relocated 2026-05-11).

- [ ] **Label-value drift is unguarded on every surface — one missing mechanism, four surfaces (2026-08-14)**: the guard pipeline validates metric *names* thoroughly and metric *label values* nowhere. `application-metrics` checks that alert-expr metrics exist in source (`crates/dt-guard/src/application_metrics.rs:424-433`), that every metric has a dashboard panel and a catalog entry; `metric-coverage` checks every emitted metric is referenced by a test. All of these match on the metric name and stop. **Nothing anywhere compares a label *value* against the set the code actually emits.** Four surfaces of the same hole, found during devloop `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-08-14-gc-meeting-refusal-causes/`:
  1. **Alert matcher ↔ emitted values.** `gc-alerts.yaml`'s `GCMeetingCreationOrgStateInvalid` selects `error_type=~"org_inactive|org_not_provisioned"`. If either value is renamed, the selector matches nothing, the rule never fires, and no guard notices — a PromQL selector that matches nothing does not error, it reports good news.
  2. **Catalog values ↔ emitted values.** `CATALOG_HEAD_RE` extracts metric names only, so the prose value list in `docs/observability/metrics/<svc>-service.md` is review-caught only. Live example found in passing: `ac-service.md:299` documents `grant_type` as "4 max — client_credentials, authorization_code, refresh_token, password" while production emits `password`, `registration`, `client_credentials`, `internal_meeting`, `internal_guest` — five values, three undocumented, two documented that nothing emits, and the stated cap is wrong.
  3. **Alert rules ↔ alert catalog.** `grep -rn "alerts\.md" scripts/ crates/dt-guard/src/` returns nothing: no guard connects `infra/docker/prometheus/rules/*.yaml` to `docs/observability/alerts.md` in either direction. The catalog silently documents only the alerts that existed when it was last written. This is the single open edge in a set where metric↔dashboard, metric↔catalog, dashboard↔kustomize and alert-metric↔source are all closed — evidence of an oversight in the guard *design*, not author sloppiness.
  4. **Dashboard panel labels ↔ catalog** — already recorded in the dashboard-set-completeness entry above ("the `dashboard-panels` guard … does NOT validate panel label keys/values against the metric catalog"). Same mechanism; fold the fixes together rather than building two.

  **Why a test does not close this.** `crates/gc-service/tests/meeting_creation_metrics_integration.rs` drives `record_meeting_creation("error", Some(<literal>), …)` from a hand-written `ALL_ERROR_TYPES` array — it never constructs the enum or calls `metric_label()`, so it pins the *wrapper*, not the vocabulary. Generalised (per @database): **a consistency check across mirrors proves nothing unless at least one mirror is tied to the ground truth.** For this vocabulary the count was four artifacts (enum, test, catalog, alert matcher) with *zero* ties to what production emits. That devloop added a real tie — `meeting_create_tests.rs` scrapes the server's live `/metrics` and asserts `after > before` per label — but only for GC meeting-creation; every other label vocabulary in the tree remains untied.

  **Prioritise by failure mode, not by cost.** A stale *matcher* fails silent (under-reports, eventually investigated); a stale *routing step or description* actively misdirects at the moment of triage, with the authority of appearing on the artifact built to answer that question. Guards reach matchers cheaply and prose not at all — the honest conclusion is that a guard would have caught one of the three drift instances found in that loop, and the other two needed a reviewer reading for meaning. Say so in the design rather than implying automation closes it. Owner: observability (with infrastructure for guard wiring). Surfaced 2026-08-14.

- [ ] **No alert rule is ever tested for whether it can fire — `promtool test rules` absent, and D-1 must not be mistaken for closing this (2026-08-14)**: `validate-alert-rules.sh` delegates to `dt-guard alert-rules-policy`, which validates that a rule is **well-formed** (`runbook_url` presence + repo-relative + on-disk target + path-traversal containment, `severity` allowlist, `for:` ≥30s floor, annotation hygiene). Nothing anywhere evaluates whether a rule *fires*. There are no rule-test fixtures and no `promtool` invocation in `scripts/` or `.github/`.

  **Demonstrated, not hypothetical.** In devloop `2026-08-14-gc-meeting-refusal-causes` an alert was authored with `for: 2h` against a condition whose maximum lifetime is ~1h15m (bounded by AC's `TOKEN_EXPIRY_SECONDS = 3600`, `crates/ac-service/src/services/token_service.rs:18`, plus the 1h `increase()` window). It was **unsatisfiable by construction** — it would have passed every guard and sat in the rules file reading as coverage while watching nothing. It was caught by a human reading it once, which is not a mechanism.

  **Reusable diagnostic worth keeping** (@database's formulation): *a `for:` clause derived from a bound that caps the condition's own lifetime is unsatisfiable by construction.* The existing `for:` check is one-sided — it enforces a floor (not too short) and has no concept of a ceiling (too long to ever be satisfied), and the unchecked side is the one that produces a silently dead rule. A smarter static check cannot close this: `for: 2h` over `increase[1h]` is perfectly legitimate for a persistent condition, and ours was unsatisfiable only because of a *domain* fact no analyser can know. Unit tests are the only mechanism; do not spend effort extending the static guard.

  **Two items, and conflating them will close this wrongly.** (a) **ADR-0034 D-1** — `dt-guard alert-rules-policy` invokes `promtool check rules` as a parallel gate; *accepted and pending*, tracked at `docs/decisions/adr-0034-guard-pipeline-as-rust-binary.md:300` as "❌ Pending | operations + observability". That is a schema/PromQL-syntax check and **would not have caught the bug above** — `for: 2h` is valid YAML containing valid PromQL. (b) **`promtool test rules` + fixtures** — undecided, unscoped, and the thing that actually closes the gap. If a future reader sees only "promtool," lands D-1 and ticks this off, the real gap stays open behind a green check. Bundle the delivery (same tool, same files, one CI wiring decision) but track the acceptance criteria separately. **Scope the first delivery to rules whose condition is expected-absent in normal operation** — those can never be validated by observation, so the risk concentrates there; `GCMeetingCreationOrgStateInvalid` is the worked example. Owner: observability + operations. Surfaced 2026-08-14.

- [ ] **Validation-pipeline timeouts are fixed wall-clock and fabricate failures under load — the apparatus as unreliable narrator (2026-08-14)**: Gate 2 attempt 2 of devloop `2026-08-14-gc-meeting-refusal-causes` returned `STATUS=FAIL REASON=guard-timeout-validate-kustomize` on a layer that takes **13s** on a quiet machine and took **713s** under concurrent `cargo` load. Nothing was wrong with the code. Per-guard timeouts are fixed thresholds with no load awareness, so a busy host manufactures a red that is **indistinguishable in the STATUS line** from a genuine one.

  **This is a distinct category from the drift entries above and belongs recorded as such.** Those are checks that can *miss* a red; this is a check that *invents* one. Both corrupt the signal, but the remedies differ and so does the damage: a fabricated failure consumes an implementer attempt, and under the Layer 7 lane policy an unlucky devloop can exhaust its attempts on host contention and escalate against a clean diff — the same defect class as R-4 (an environment failure recorded as an implementer failure), one layer up. Note the existing sibling at the top of this file: AC's timing-attack test is wall-clock flaky under parallel `#[sqlx::test]` load for the same underlying reason, so this is a pattern in the harness, not a one-off.

  **Options, not a decision**: load-aware or adaptive timeouts; a distinct STATUS reason that separates "timed out" from "failed" at the lane level so it routes to the operator lane rather than the implementer's; or serialising the pipeline against concurrent build jobs. Whichever, the requirement is that a timeout must not be reportable as an implementer-lane failure. Owner: operations (harness) with observability (signal semantics). Surfaced 2026-08-14 at Gate 2.

- [ ] **GC availability SLO counts 4xx in its error numerator — role denials burn the error budget (2026-08-14)**: `GCHighErrorRate`, `GCErrorBudgetBurnRateCritical` and `GCErrorBudgetBurnRateWarning` in `infra/docker/prometheus/rules/gc-alerts.yaml` all use `status_code=~"[45].."`. Every 401/403 — an unauthenticated probe, a caller lacking a meeting-create role, a guest hitting `allow_guests=false` — therefore burns the 99.9% availability budget and can page. Client-attributable rejections are the service working correctly. Surfaced during R-6 when an implementer measured, correctly, that moving a cause from 403 to 500 "does not change any alert numerator" — true, and true *because* the numerator is miscalibrated; that measurement should not become the precedent that status class is semantically free. **Not fixed in that devloop**: changing an SLO numerator is a real semantic change to a shipped signal (it moves the error-budget baseline and the burn-rate history becomes non-comparable), so it needs its own scoping rather than riding a handler change. Likely shape: numerator on `5..` plus explicitly-chosen 4xx classes, if any. Owner: observability. Surfaced 2026-08-14.

- [ ] **The five ADR-0036 stream/key identifiers have no mechanical metric-label PII guard after the `user_id`→`sender_id` rename (owner: dt-guard tooling for the guard change; story R-29 for the constraint)** (found 2026-09-01 by @observability at Gate 3 of `2026-08-31-signaling-media-contract-reshape`): `user_id` is in `crates/dt-guard/src/common/pii_vocabulary.rs::PII_TOKENS_CATEGORY_B` and was blocked as a metric label by `metric_labels.rs:920`; the rename silently removed that coverage. `sender_id`, `slot_id`, `stream_number`, `kek_generation` and `switch_command_id` are NOT in any set, and `token_hit_in_set` splits on `_`, so neither `sender` nor `id` matches — a control→convention downgrade in the same diff whose `build.rs` argues "this is a control, not a convention". **The guard change itself is deferred** (needs a new non-PII cardinality category, is shared gate tooling, and the contamination argument for keeping gate-tooling changes out of a feature diff applies). **Not deferrable — the tracking**: the ADR-0036 §11 never-a-metric-label / never-a-span-attribute / never-a-per-frame-log-dimension prohibition for these five spellings currently lives ONLY in `proto/dark_tower/signaling/v1/signaling.proto` field comments, and story R-29 points at R-24's abstract categories, so the R-29 implementer has no way to find the concrete list. This bullet IS that list.

- [ ] **`metric-coverage` silently DISCARDS a metric name it cannot parse instead of failing on it, so a hoisted `const` name produces a clean scan rather than a violation (found 2026-09-01 by @observability at Gate 1 of `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-09-01-mh-transport-seam/`, prompted by @paired-media-handler's task-16 registration-pattern question; owner: observability per ADR-0031, implementation dt-guard/infrastructure)**. `crates/dt-guard/src/metric_coverage.rs:extract_emission_metric_names()` post-filters each extracted first argument against `^[a-z][a-z0-9_]+$` and `continue`s on a miss. That filter was added to discard stray regex captures, and for that purpose it is correct — but it cannot distinguish "stray capture" from "a real `counter!` invocation whose name is an identifier rather than a literal". A registration written as `counter!(MH_EGRESS_DROPS, ...)` with `const MH_EGRESS_DROPS: &str` therefore vanishes from the coverage set **and reports clean** — a false negative wearing the appearance of a scan. Same failure shape as the guard-path-roots entry above: an unparseable input is treated as an empty result rather than as an error. The hazard is not hypothetical and is about to become live: ADR-0036 §11 requires media-path metrics to use **cached handles** resolved once at setup with no macro reachable from the forward function, which is satisfiable *and* guard-visible only if registration stays a string literal inside `crates/mh-service/src/observability/metrics.rs` (`metrics` 0.24.6's `counter!`/`gauge!`/`histogram!` return the handle — "can be held on to in order to amortize the cost of registration"). Hoisting the name to a `const` is the change a reviewer would read as an *improvement* while it blinds the guard, which is what makes this worth a mechanical fix rather than a convention. **Fix**: when the macro-invocation regex matches but the first argument is not a string literal, emit a violation ("metric name must be a string literal at the registration site") instead of `continue`. Distinguishing the two cases needs the first-arg parser to report *why* it rejected — stray capture versus non-literal — so this is a small parser change plus a new rule id, not a one-line edit; fixture coverage belongs in `crates/dt-guard/tests/fixtures/`. Applies to every service, not just MH. @paired-media-handler has committed to the literal-in-`metrics.rs` pattern for task 16, so nothing is broken today — this closes the path by which a later refactor breaks it silently.

- [ ] **Task manifest prompts are a second encoding of requirements with no mechanical binding — three instances, one mechanism (surfaced 2026-09-02, story task 8, security review)**: `scripts/workflow/run-story.sh` feeds a task's manifest `prompt:` to its implementer. That prompt restates requirements owned elsewhere in the same story file, and **nothing binds the two**, so a correction to the requirement never reaches the instruction that is actually executed. Three instances on one story: **(1)** task 10's prompt contradicted the shipped `JoinResponse` contract (recorded above, corrected 2026-09-01); **(2)** task 15's prompt required an Assumption-4 carry it did not state, corrected in task 8; **(3)** task 19's prompt pinned `replay`, omitted `unwrap_failed`, and instructed `decode_reject` as a `reason` value — which **R-25 at line 53 of the same file forbids verbatim**, a correction litigated on 2026-08-31 and invisible from line 420. Note instance (3) is *intra-file*: both the correction and its stale derivative live in the same document, so a reader of either alone gets a confident, internally consistent answer. **The general rule this is an instance of**: a second encoding is a liability unless it has an audience the first cannot serve **and** a bidirectional mechanical check binding the two — one failing on divergence in either direction, since a check detecting only additions lets the second copy silently shrink. Where the audience is real but no mechanical check is possible, cite rather than restate: a pointer dangles loudly, a restatement goes stale silently. Manifest prompts satisfy the audience leg (the runner genuinely needs them) and fail the check leg, which is exactly why they go stale while `frame-v2.vectors.json` — same duplication shape, bound by g5 set-equality — does not. **Fix is not "stop duplicating"**; it is supplying the missing leg. Feasibility is real but the work is scoping, not the check: tokens are backticked in both prompt and requirement, so extraction is tractable, but a naive `` `[a-z_]+` `` sweep hits `dt_client_media_frames_dropped_total` (a metric name) alongside `no_kek_for_generation` (a reason), so it needs a convention marking which backticked strings are vocabulary references. **Owner: OPEN — do not default it to the finders.** This is ADR-0035 / run-story territory, and the owner is **undiscoverable from four sources**: ADR-0035 has no Participants/Deciders section, `scripts/workflow/run-story.sh` and `crates/dt-story/` name no specialist across their commit history, no `.claude/agents/*.md` references either path, and `scripts/guards/simple/cross-boundary-ownership.yaml` — the authoritative registry — has no entry for either. ADR-0035 §11 places story-runner artifacts outside observability's *monitoring* regime (directional, not dispositive: it excludes ADR-0032 governance from the **run record**, which is not this). ADR-0035 §7 treats an undeclared owner as a fail-loud condition in the one place it addresses ownership — precedent for the principle, **not** an instance of the rule, which qualifies a red *language*. Raised with @team-lead at task 8 verdict. **Adjacent**: the Start Review gate has the same shape — two identically-briefed reviewers with identical pre-registration diverged on the same completion ping, demonstrating the gate is not self-enforcing. Same likely owner, same undiscoverable-owner wall; whoever picks up one should take both.

## Documentation Hygiene

- [ ] **The `hear-yourself-through-handler` story text asserts a kustomize verification against a version this tree does not run (2026-09-02)**: `docs/user-stories/2026-08-27-hear-yourself-through-handler.md:104` says the `MH_TERMINATION_GRACE_SECONDS` derivation was "verified against kustomize v5.7.1: deleting the source field fails the build". There is **no standalone `kustomize` binary** in the devloop container; both consumers of `infra/services/mh-service/` are `kubectl` — the guard (`crates/dt-guard/src/kustomize.rs` via `scripts/guards/simple/validate-kustomize.sh`) and the deploy path (`infra/kind/scripts/setup.sh:1208`) — and `kubectl v1.32.3` embeds **Kustomize v5.5.0**. Task #9 re-verified all three cases (happy path, source field deleted, target env entry deleted) on v5.5.0 and the hard-fail holds in both directions, so the *design* is sound; only the cited version is wrong. Recorded rather than corrected in place because the story file is the run-story manifest under ADR-0035 and editing it mid-story is not a task's call (Lead ruling, task #9 Gate 1). Owner: whoever closes the story. Defer trigger: story close, or any future task citing that verification.

- [ ] **AC's shutdown comments cite ADR-0012 for a 30s drain period that ADR-0012 does not contain (2026-09-02)**: `crates/ac-service/src/main.rs:171` ("ADR-0012: 30s graceful shutdown drain period") and `:188` ("ADR-0012: Graceful shutdown with 30s drain period") both attribute the figure to ADR-0012, which says nothing about drain, grace, or shutdown. Surfaced by @operations at task #9 Gate 1 while auditing the drain-window sibling class; the erroneous citation was about to be propagated into a devloop record before being caught. This is an unsupported citation rather than a wrong number — AC's actual drain behaviour is not in question. Owner: **auth-controller** (cross-owner; task #9 was manifest-only and did not touch AC source). Defer trigger: next auth-controller touch to `main.rs`, or any task relying on the ADR-0012 attribution.

- [ ] **`docs/runbooks/mh-incident-response.md` is systemically stale on the MC/MH per-instance split — ~35 commands that cannot run (2026-09-02, story task #9)**: there is no `mh-service` Deployment (`kubectl get deploy mh-service -n dark-tower` → `NotFound`); MH runs as per-instance Deployments `mh-0` and `mh-1`. The file still says `deployment/mh-service` at **35 sites**; re-derive them with `grep -n 'deployment/mh-service' docs/runbooks/mh-incident-response.md` rather than trusting a frozen list. **A line-number enumeration was deliberately removed from this entry** (@operations, 2026-09-02): it was captured mid-commit and was already wrong when filed — 16 of its 41 numbers had shifted, 10 real sites were missing, and it cited `:914`, which task #9's own Scenario 14 fix had just replaced, so a reader working the list top-down would have been pointed at re-breaking the fix. The file is the source of truth; a line-number list is a second encoding of it that drifts on the next edit — here, in under one commit. Fix the class by grep, not by line number. Includes a second unimplementable `kubectl scale deployment/mh-service --replicas=5` (in the pod-restart scenario) (per the `Per-instance NodePort Services` comment block in `infra/services/mh-service/service.yaml`, changing instance count requires adding NodePort Services, Kind port mappings, per-instance Deployments and ConfigMaps — scaling is a manifest change, not an incident-time lever), and several `kubectl exec -it deployment/mh-service -- openssl ...` cert inspections that are doubly broken: wrong mount path (`/certs` vs the actual `/etc/mh-tls` — see the `mh-tls` volumeMount in `mh-{0,1}-deployment.yaml`; do not cite a line number, task #9's own edit to that file moved it by 54 lines) and a distroless image with no shell. **Scope note — Scenario 14's Option B block and its recovery-time estimate were FIXED by task #9 and are NOT part of this entry**; so were Scenario 5's Impact/Immediate/root-causes/Recovery/cert-inspection blocks. **Deliberately left in place, and this is the rule that drew the line**: a stale *name* fails loudly and safely (`NotFound` costs a minute), whereas a destructive instruction succeeds and lies — `kubectl delete secret mh-service-tls` destroys the only copy of MH's TLS identity, after which the runbook told the responder to wait for a cert-manager re-issuance that never comes. Only the second class cleared the bar for in-scope repair. Two descriptive mislabels also remain by the same rule: the *Kubernetes / cloud* dependency line's `TLS cert-manager` (possibly aspirational-for-production) and Scenario 14's Common Root Cause 1 ("cert-manager Secret missing"). Re-derive both with `grep -n cert-manager docs/runbooks/mh-incident-response.md`. There is **no cert-manager** in this system: `grep -rln cert-manager infra/ scripts/` returns zero hits, and the Secret is created imperatively by `infra/kind/scripts/setup.sh:create_mh_tls_secret()`. **Fix shape**: this is not a name substitution — several commands need a judgment about what the correct instruction *should be*, not just a corrected Deployment name. The `kubectl scale --replicas=5` sites need deleting outright (scaling is not an incident-time lever for MH, per `service.yaml`'s own note), and the `kubectl exec ... openssl` cert inspections need replacing with the read-the-Secret-from-outside form that Scenario 5 now uses, because the image is distroless. (The cert delete-and-wait that would also have qualified was task #9's in-scope fix and no longer exists — `grep -n 'delete secret' docs/runbooks/mh-incident-response.md` returns nothing.) Check `docs/runbooks/mc-incident-response.md` for the same class; MC has the identical per-instance shape. Owner: **operations**. Defer trigger: next MH incident, or the next touch to either incident-response runbook.

- [ ] **Reconcile the secure-context prose duplicated between `scripts/dev-web.sh` and `docs/runbooks/client-dev-local.md` once story task 20 lands** (added 2026-09-01, devloop `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-09-01-release-premise-and-preflight-guards/`; surfaced by @dry-reviewer as F-DRY-E; owner `infrastructure` + `client`). The `dev-web.sh` header carries an inline secure-context summary — the four gated APIs, `.localhost`/loopback being potentially trustworthy, and non-loopback HTTP presenting as "joined, no audio". Three of those four statements are also mandated content for task 20's `## Secure Context and Media Setup` runbook section, so when task 20 lands there will be **two copies**. The inline copy is deliberate, not an oversight: @security's argument is that a reader who follows a not-yet-existing anchor is by construction someone who just hit a hard fail, which is exactly the moment a prohibited insecure-origin flag gets pasted in — so the substance must be at the point of failure during the window where the target does not exist. **The window closes when task 20 lands**; at that point decide whether the header keeps a one-line summary plus the pointer, or the pointer alone. Two sites: `scripts/dev-web.sh` (header, SECURE CONTEXT block) and `docs/runbooks/client-dev-local.md#secure-context-and-media-setup`. Partial forcing function already in place — `scripts/dev-web.test.sh`'s `secure-context-anchor-resolves` fires the moment any secure-context heading appears and pins its slug to the cited anchor, so a mis-spelled landing reds; it does **not** detect prose divergence, which is what this entry is for.

- [ ] **`/user-story` has no ADR — its two sibling workflows do**: ADR-0024 (Agent Teams Development Workflow) documents §1 the dev-loop and §2 the debate workflow and never mentions `/user-story`; ADR-0022 is superseded by it; ADR-0035 covers the runner that *executes* stories, not the planning that produces them. So the design decisions behind a 12-specialist planning workflow — all-specialists-always, the architecture gate, the 3-round limit, continue mode — exist only as mechanism in `.claude/skills/user-story/SKILL.md`, with no record of why. If written, it is the right home for the requirement-altitude rule, which should **move** there from ADR-0035 §13 (a general planning principle currently filed in a story-runner ADR, where nobody planning a user-facing story will find it) rather than being copied. Low urgency — the workflow works and the skill is the behavioural SSoT; this is a *why* gap, not a *how* gap. Surfaced 2026-08-15.

- [ ] **ADR-0010 §4a describes "max 3 retries before returning 503" for MC rejections, now refined without a forward-pointer (surfaced 2026-08-21 by @code-reviewer at Gate 3 of `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-08-21-gc-invalid-request-rejection-reason/`)**: that devloop added `REJECTION_REASON_INVALID_REQUEST` and made GC fail fast (500, no pool-walk) on a GC-verified malformed selection — a deliberate, sanctioned narrowing of §4a's retry model (the fail-fast/vs-retry call was explicitly delegated to the implementer per the devloop's Debate Decision; proto, not the ADR, is the `RejectionReason` enum SSoT). Post-S-3 the deviation is narrow: the full pool-walk failover is preserved for every case EXCEPT a GC-verified contract violation. A future reader of §4a's "max 3 retries → 503" prose would not learn of the `invalid_request` fail-fast/500 exception. **Fix**: a one-line forward-pointer in ADR-0010's implementation-status table (or §4a) noting the new variant + the narrow fail-fast exception and citing the devloop. Not folded into the carrier devloop: editing a shipped ADR is its own governance judgment and would add an out-of-plan file to a finalized changeset. Owner: global-controller (+ protocol if the wire-contract framing is touched). Defer trigger: next devloop touching ADR-0010 or GC assignment retry semantics. Low urgency — the devloop record documents the decision thoroughly; this is a *discoverability* gap in the ADR.

- [ ] **Knowledge-index 75-line cap is Goodharting INDEX curation** (surfaced in devloop `2026-07-29-client-credential-lifetime-guard-task58`; filed 2026-08-03 by Lead with attribution per that loop's §Accepted Deferrals instruction): six of fifteen `docs/specialist-knowledge/*/INDEX.md` files sit at exactly the 75-line cap, so "merge two lines to make room" is becoming the coping strategy — line-merging optimizes the metric (line count) at the expense of the goal (scannable navigation). Options to evaluate: raise the cap, cap by byte/entry count instead, or allow per-specialist overrides with justification. Owner: @paired-infrastructure-2 (infrastructure). Source: `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-07-29-client-credential-lifetime-guard-task58/main.md` §Accepted Deferrals.

- [x] **Devloop-output template wording — parentheticals/globs now tolerated**: `docs/devloop-outputs/_template/main.md:78-85` instructs plan authors "bare backtick-quoted filename only — no parentheticals, no globs". As of the 2026-05-08 Layer A scope-drift parser fix (R-62, ADR-0033 Wave 1 #3), the parser DOES tolerate trailing-parenthetical annotations and globs (`*`/`?`/`[`/`**`). The wording update is a one-line edit, but the discussion ("should we *recommend* parentheticals/globs or just *tolerate* them?") deserves its own micro-decision. Spin out as a small follow-up devloop. Owner: operations + code-reviewer. Source: `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-05-08-layer-a-scope-drift-parser-fix/main.md` Tech Debt References. (resolved 2026-05-08 via devloop-outputs/2026-05-08-glob-trailing-slash-and-template-wording)

- [ ] **gc-deployment.md `mcAssignment.mcUrl` references stale (no such field on McAssignmentInfo)** (2026-05-23): `docs/runbooks/gc-deployment.md:898, 900, 905, 933` reference `.mcAssignment.mcUrl` / "MC URL" in jq paths and prose. The actual `crates/gc-service/src/models/mod.rs::McAssignmentInfo` (L227) exposes `mc_id`, `webtransport_endpoint`, and `grpc_endpoint` — **no `mc_url` field exists or ever existed** in the wire schema. Pre-existing runbook bug; the R-53 camelCase migration mechanically flipped `mc_url` → `mcUrl` but did not introduce the references. Fix: either remove the `mcUrl` references entirely or expand to `webtransportEndpoint`/`grpcEndpoint` matching the actual response shape. Owner: global-controller. Surfaced 2026-05-23 by @code-reviewer Gate 2 review of devloop `2026-05-23-camelcase-wire-migration`.

- [ ] **dt-guard `rust-no-hardcoded-secrets` brace-counter latches onto wrong `{` after top-of-file `#[cfg(test)] use ...;`** (2026-05-23): The Rust secret-scan guard's `compute_test_block_ranges` walks forward from any `#[cfg(test)]` attribute looking for the first `{` to identify a test-block opener. On `crates/ac-service/src/handlers/auth_handler.rs` (and likely any file with a top-of-file `#[cfg(test)] use crate::config::X;` followed by another `use` block with braces), the walker latches onto the `use axum::{...}` block at L10-14 rather than the actual `#[cfg(test)] mod tests { ... }` block at L350-1078. Effect: tests inside the production-file's `mod tests` get scanned despite the existing exclusion logic. Workaround used in R-53: indirect `<secret_word>: "..."` field-literal assignments through a non-trigger-named `let` binding so the regex's `[=:]\s*"` requirement isn't met. Underlying fix options: (a) require the brace-walker to stop at the next non-attribute non-use statement, (b) only treat a `{` as the test-block opener when preceded by `mod` on the same or prior line, (c) skip `#[cfg(test)]` attributes that precede `use` items by detecting the `use` keyword before the search starts. Single-file change in `crates/dt-guard/src/common/test_code_filter.rs`. Owner: operations + test (dt-guard maintainers). Surfaced 2026-05-23 in devloop `2026-05-23-camelcase-wire-migration` Gate 2 Issue 2.

- [ ] **Remove status/tracking content from all ADRs — pivoted to user stories**: At least 9 ADRs (`adr-0003`, `adr-0005`, `adr-0006`, `adr-0007`, `adr-0009`, `adr-0010`, `adr-0012`, `adr-0020`, `adr-0023`) carry "Phase X" tables, `❌ Pending` checklists, or `Status | Commit` columns documenting implementation progress. Status tracking has since pivoted to user stories (`docs/user-stories/`); ADRs are now design records only. The status content is stale, conflicts with current user-story tracking, and creates maintenance traps when implementation evolves (e.g., ADR-0023 §Phase 6h still lists `mc_errors_total` as a "Pending" requirement after the metric was removed). Sweep all ADRs: delete status tables, phase checklists, "Status / Commit" columns, and standalone "Implementation Status" sections. Keep only design content: context, decision, rationale, consequences, alternatives. Cross-reference user stories where the implementation is tracked. Owner: code-reviewer + observability (ADR-0023 / 0029 / 0011 are observability-touching). Per-ADR survey to identify which sections are status vs design before sweeping. Likely a multi-devloop effort given ADR count.

- [x] **`scripts/lang/proto/changed.test.sh:18-20` stale "real file" claim post-proto-move**: After task #30 moved the proto files into `proto/dark_tower/{internal,signaling}/`, the synthetic test inputs `proto/internal.proto` and `proto/signaling.proto` at these lines no longer exist on disk. The predicate-classifier uses a prefix match (`diff_touches_path "proto/"`), so the assertions still pass — but the inline comment claiming these are "real files in the repo (catches predicate-pattern-regression)" is now wrong. Resolved 2026-05-20 via the reword option: comment now reads "Path strings matched via `diff_touches_path \"proto/\"` — prefix-only, file existence is not checked. Inputs are deliberately a mix of synthetic paths and real config files; both exercise the same prefix pattern any regression would break." Source: code-reviewer Finding 2 at `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-05-20-proto-file-layout-task30/main.md`.

- [ ] **infrastructure/INDEX.md:42 — `§6.7` inside backticks trips knowledge-index stale_pointer** (surfaced 2026-06-29 at task #14 Gate 2; PRE-EXISTING — present at f34f3a6, introduced by #56 fill-layer7): the section ref sits INSIDE the backticks (`` `docs/runbooks/devloop-validation.md §6.7` ``). `validate-knowledge-index` (`knowledge_index.rs:52` strip_suffixes) strips `:line`/`:Symbol`/`#anchor` but not a space-delimited `§6.7`, so it resolves a non-existent path; the file AND §6.7 (runbook line 466) both exist — a citation-convention violation, not a dead pointer. **Fix**: close the backtick after `.md` (`` ...mapping -> `docs/runbooks/devloop-validation.md` §6.7; Lead... ``). Zero domain risk. Reds the Layer-3 pipeline for EVERY devloop on this branch until fixed (separate commit, not in a feature diff). Owner: infrastructure. Optional follow-up (operations): harden `strip_suffixes` to tolerate ` §N.N`. Root-caused by @operations at task #14 Gate 2.

- [ ] **`knowledge_index.rs` checks only 4 path prefixes — 31% of all INDEX pointers are never verified** (surfaced 2026-08-10 during the ADR-0035 debate reflection step; measured independently by @code-reviewer and @infrastructure, agreeing): `is_in_scope_path` (`crates/dt-guard/src/knowledge_index.rs:89-93`) admits only `docs/`, `crates/`, `proto/`, `scripts/`. Across the 15 `docs/specialist-knowledge/*/INDEX.md` files: **996 backticked pointers, 686 checked, 310 (31%) never examined** — led by `infra/` (88), then bare relative forms that match no prefix (`src/` 21, `tests/` 15, `handlers/` 7, `actors/` 6, `grpc/`, `middleware/` — 57 total), plus `.claude/` (14), `packages/` (4) and root-level files (`package.json`, `Cargo.toml`, `clippy.toml`, `audit-suppressions.toml`). **The 57 are the more dangerous half even though 88 is the bigger one**: `infra/` at least looks like a top-level tree an author might wonder about, whereas `handlers/auth_handler.rs` reads as a perfectly normal in-repo pointer — nothing about it signals "unverified." Consequence: for seats whose domain lives outside the four prefixes — **infrastructure most of all, where most pointers are `infra/`** — "keep pointers fresh" is a convention with no mechanism behind it, inside the guard suite built to replace conventions with mechanisms. **Why task-sized, not a one-line fix**: widening the predicate brings 310 unverified pointers under the guard *at once* across 14 specialists' files, with no advance knowledge of how many are already stale — a coordinated red-then-green migration (measure staleness first, fix per-owner, then widen), not a predicate edit. Doing it carelessly reds Layer 3 branch-wide for everyone. Owner: infrastructure (ADR-0034 guard pipeline). Separate commit, not in a feature diff. **Proven cost, not theoretical**: expanding one out-of-scope brace form during this debate exposed `infra/services/{mc,mh}-service/deployment.yaml` as **stale at HEAD** — invisible in both directions, since brace notation over an out-of-scope prefix is unreadable to the guard either way. The real files are per-instance (`mc-0-deployment.yaml`/`mc-1-deployment.yaml`, `mh-0-*`/`mh-1-*`), which is the **same per-instance-filename assumption already documented for `find_workload()`** in the §Infrastructure Validation in Devloops entry — second artifact, same root cause; cross-reference rather than re-file. **Severity is higher than "a stale pointer": the same broken pointer was replicated across THREE specialists' navigation files — media-handler, meeting-controller and observability — two of whom were not in the debate and would never have looked.** All three named a `deployment.yaml` that has never existed, for an unknown duration, and it surfaced only because one person expanded a brace form the guard never reads. A navigation index pointing three specialists at a file that has never existed is exactly the failure the guard exists to prevent, sitting in the 31% it does not examine — which makes `infra/` unambiguously the right first prefix for the staged widening, now on evidence rather than pointer count alone. **And the blind spot is not merely historical decay — it actively accumulates new defects.** A fourth stale pointer, `infra/kind/setup.sh` (real path `infra/kind/scripts/setup.sh`), appears in **no** INDEX file at HEAD: it was *introduced during this same reflection step*, by the specialist who had just measured the blind spot, minutes after telling four teammates that `infra/` pointers pass whether or not they are real. Verified against HEAD. The author then certified the file clean twice on a `STATUS=OK` that only ever covered the 64 in-scope pointers of its 75 — a green result read as an answer to a question it was not answering. **That is a stronger argument for widening the predicate than any count of unchecked pointers**, because it shows the unchecked region accumulates defects even from authors who know it is unchecked. (All four fixed — the three above, plus this one. Note `infra/services/gc-service/deployment.yaml` is **legitimate** — GC is a stateless Deployment; only MC/MH are per-instance. A regex sweep for `service/deployment.yaml` false-positives on it.) **Design hazard to carry into the widening pass**: the cheapest way to satisfy this guard is to *delete* pointers — observed live, when an interim repair cleared violations by dropping `gc/mc/mh` from a metrics pointer and reducing file enumerations to bare directories; the owners caught it and restored the content. Guard-clean but lossy, and the 75-line cap pushes the same direction, so the cap and the resolve-check need considering together.

- [ ] **`knowledge_index.rs` misdiagnoses brace/placeholder shorthand as a stale pointer** (same surfacing as above): `is_glob_or_placeholder` (`knowledge_index.rs:95-97`) exempts `*` and `NNNN` but not `{a,b}` or `<placeholder>`, so an in-scope `crates/foo/src/{a,b}.rs` is read as one literal filename and fails **even when every member exists**. Four INDEX files tripped this in one reflection step. **The defect is the diagnostic, not the rejection**: `:166-167` reports it under `STALE_POINTER_RULE_ID`, which tells an author *"this file moved"* and sends them hunting a rename when the fault is *"this syntax is unsupported"* — the same misattribution class as the runner collapsing an infra-lane exit into an implementer-blaming `pipeline-red`. **Fix**: keep rejecting shorthand; add a fifth rule ID beside the four at `:17-20` (established pattern, not new machinery) — `UNSUPPORTED_SHORTHAND_RULE_ID = "unsupported_shorthand"`, message *"brace/placeholder shorthand is not resolved — write full literal paths, or point at the directory"* — and **name the specific unresolved member**. **Two alternatives considered and rejected**: *exempting* `{` converts a loud failure into a quiet one (CLAUDE.md "fail loudly; never mask"); *expanding* the brace list is a **convention reversal** — the debate settled on "full literal paths, no shorthand" and all 15 INDEX files were fixed to it, so teaching the resolver to expand re-legitimizes the shorthand the group just agreed to drop. Keeping the rejection satisfies both constraints at once. Related: `strip_suffixes` also mishandles a `:{a,b}()` multi-symbol annotation (`crates/devloop-helper/src/commands.rs`) and ` §N.N` (entry above) — at three distinct mishandled forms, **the function is the finding, not the individual cases**. Owner: infrastructure.

- [ ] **Reflection step should run `dt-guard knowledge-index --root .` before reporting done** (surfaced 2026-08-10, ADR-0035 debate; proposed by @code-reviewer, endorsed by @infrastructure): the guard is correct and Layer 3 runs it, but nothing gates the interval between authoring an INDEX and committing it — six specialists edited in parallel under a 75-line cap and four turned Layer 3 red simultaneously. The cap actively incentivises the compression that breaks the guard. **Fix**: add the command to the reflection instruction itself (one command, already in the pipeline, moved to where the judgment happens). The instruction is the only thing an author reads at that moment. Owner: whoever owns the reflection prompt.

- [ ] **2026-06-26-fill-layer7-env-tests/main.md:312-315 — §Accepted Deferrals numbered paragraphs trip todo-tracking inline_debt_body** (surfaced 2026-06-29 at task #14 Gate 2; PRE-EXISTING — introduced by #56): `validate-todo-tracking` (`todo_tracking.rs:99`) treats only `-`/`*`/`+` as bullets, not numbered `1.`/`2.`; the #56 §Accepted Deferrals uses numbered entries with multi-sentence inline bodies → trips at line 315. **Fix**: rewrite §Accepted Deferrals (312-315) from numbered paragraphs to terse dash-bullet POINTERS into `docs/TODO.md` (the doc already cites `docs/TODO.md §Env-Test Resilience`) — real one-line pointers, NOT a cosmetic `1.`→`- ` swap. Both entries are already-resolved/tracked, so pure reformat, no content loss. Reds the Layer-3 pipeline branch-wide. Owner: doc-hygiene / #56 output owner. Root-caused by @operations at task #14 Gate 2.

- [ ] **ADR-0036 §10 Tier 1a says "All deadline logic takes an injected clock", which reads as mandating a `Clock` trait this workspace deliberately does not have (authored by @code-reviewer, raised by @dry-reviewer as D3 and adjudicated at Gate 1 of `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-09-01-mh-transport-seam/`; landed by @test as the file's editor that loop; owner: code-reviewer)**. There is no `Clock` trait anywhere in the tree. The established deterministic-time idiom is tokio's paused timer — `tokio::time::{pause, advance, sleep, sleep_until, Instant}` under a `start_paused` runtime — used at `crates/mc-service/src/actors/meeting.rs`, `crates/mh-service/src/webtransport/connection.rs`, and the mc-service integration suites. The ADR sentence names a **property** (a deadline must be drivable from a test, never wall-clock-dependent), and a test runtime substituting virtual time at the *runtime* boundary satisfies that property as fully as a trait at the *type* boundary would, without adding a parallel mechanism to a working one. Two tasks in the ADR-0036 story already resolve it that way in their own prompts, in those words. **Proposed reword**: replace "All deadline logic takes an injected clock" with "All deadline logic must be drivable from a test without wall-clock dependence — in this workspace via `tokio::time` pause/advance under a `start_paused` runtime; a `Clock` trait is not required and none exists." **Why an entry and not an edit**: amending a shipped, Accepted ADR is its own governance judgment and does not belong inside a feature changeset; the risk being tracked is that the literal sentence invites a future implementer to introduce a `Clock` trait as *compliance*, creating the second mechanism the workspace has so far avoided. Low urgency, non-blocking — every affected implementation already does the right thing.

- [ ] **The DRY reviewer's own verdict rule is stated five times across two incompatible models, and two of the statements return different verdicts for the identical state (filed 2026-09-01 by @dry-reviewer at Gate 3 of `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-09-01-mh-transport-seam/`, at @team-lead's direction after the conflict forced a judgement call at that gate; owner: whoever owns ADR-0019 — code-reviewer or operations as protocol owner)**. **The question, stated plainly, because it is the thing that must be answered before any edit**: *does a tracked cross-service extraction entry — which by construction is not a finding and is not in the diff — set the reviewer's verdict to RESOLVED-DEFERRED, or leave it CLEAR?* **The two statements that collide**, both in `.claude/skills/devloop/review-protocol.md`: `:149` says "The verdict is **RESOLVED-DEFERRED** if *any* finding remains **in the diff** after this PR", which an extraction opportunity never does — it describes code elsewhere, and `:118` explicitly rules that extraction opportunities "do NOT enter the fix-or-defer flow"; while `:157` says §Accepted Deferrals collects "accepted deferrals, spin-outs, **or DRY extraction opportunities**" and "If this section is non-empty, your verdict above is RESOLVED-DEFERRED." One keys on *findings in the diff*, the other on *section contents including non-findings*. For a review with zero outstanding findings and one filed extraction entry — the exact state at the 2026-09-01 seam devloop — `:149` yields CLEAR and `:157` yields RESOLVED-DEFERRED. **The root cause is a superseded severity model that was never marked superseded**, which is why this reads as three statements rather than one bug. ADR-0019 defines a severity taxonomy — `:44-47` (🔴 BLOCKING / 📋 TECH_DEBT), `:49` ("All findings block EXCEPT TECH_DEBT"), `:39`, `:60`, `:63` ("could be extracted to common = Ship now, refactor later (non-blocking)"), `:87` ("Doesn't block velocity") — and `review-protocol.md:78` states the opposite in terms: "**Every finding defaults to 'fix it.'** There are no severity levels", reinforced at `:93` ("severity is not deferral justification"). The fix-or-defer model replaced the severity model in practice, but **ADR-0019 carries no Status line and no supersession marker**, so it reads as live; and `review-protocol.md:117-118`'s "DRY Reviewer Exception" is the bridge clause that carried one limb of the old model into the new verdict taxonomy, which has no category for a non-finding that still costs something. **Fifth site, and the one that actually drives behaviour**: `.claude/agents/dry-reviewer.md:23-24,44,48` — the agent definition the reviewer is configured from — speaks **only** the superseded BLOCKER/TECH_DEBT language and does not mention the verdict taxonomy at all. So the operator of the rule is instructed in the older model while being graded in the newer one; that asymmetry is the reason this surfaced as a Gate-3 judgement call rather than as an obvious doc typo. **Both answers are defensible and the entry deliberately does not pick one**, because the reviewer the rule governs should not be the one who resolves it in their own favour: (a) *extraction entries do not set the verdict* is faithful to ADR-0019's "doesn't block velocity" intent and to `:149`'s in-the-diff wording, and keeps CLEAR meaning "this diff is clean", but it makes a real cost shift invisible at the gate; (b) *they do set it* is faithful to `:157` and to the verdict split's stated purpose ("a deferral cannot hide inside a resolution"), but it means a DRY reviewer can essentially never return CLEAR, since filing extraction entries is the role's normal output — which drains the signal from the distinction. A third option worth considering rather than defaulting: a distinct verdict or suffix for "no findings, extraction tracked", so the two states stay separable instead of one absorbing the other. **@team-lead ruled (b) at the 2026-09-01 gate**, explicitly taking the less flattering reading and declining to override down to CLEAR on the technicality; that ruling is precedent for the interim but is not a substitute for amending the docs. **Fix when acted on — amend every site in one change, or the drift simply relocates**: `docs/decisions/adr-0019-dry-reviewer.md` (add a Status/supersession note for the severity taxonomy, or restate it in fix-or-defer terms), `.claude/skills/devloop/review-protocol.md:118`, `:147`, `:149`, `:157`, and `.claude/agents/dry-reviewer.md:23-24,44,48`. Note `:149` and `:157` are ~8 lines apart in the same section, which is how they diverged unnoticed. **Defer trigger**: the next DRY verdict that files an extraction entry with no outstanding findings — i.e. most of them.

## Story Workflow Follow-ups (run-story v1 lessons)

- [ ] **A devloop cannot land as more than one commit, so a "revert the gate only" rollback path
  cannot be built** (surfaced 2026-09-02 by @team-lead during story task 8, attempting the
  three-commit ordering @operations required as OPS-13 and @team-lead granted at Gate 3). Two
  independent mechanisms each bind a devloop's validation to a single commit against the pre-devloop
  HEAD, and they compound: the Gate-2 pre-commit signature binding
  (`scripts/lang/_gate2_binding.sh:710`) recomputes over the whole set changed vs HEAD, so the first
  commit invalidates the verdict for every later one; re-running `layer-all.sh` to refresh the
  signature then reds Layer 3, because `validate-cross-boundary-scope` reports
  `scope_drift_planned_untouched` for the paths the first commit just landed — they are in the
  plan's classification table and no longer in the effective diff. **Why it matters beyond
  tidiness:** OPS-13's separable-gate commit is the standard mitigation whenever a devloop adds an
  always-run, deliberately non-suppressible Layer-3 gate — with no suppression switch by design, a
  clean revert is the *only* escape hatch. The runbook already records the pattern as established
  practice for the `no-retained-credentials` guard, so this is a documented practice the tooling
  cannot currently support. It is not fixable at a commit gate: both mechanisms are correct for the
  single-commit case they were written for, and the fix is a decision about whether a devloop's
  verdict and scope should bind to a *commit range* rather than to HEAD — ADR-0033 (Gate-2 binding,
  scope guard) and ADR-0035 (devloop→commit relationship) territory. Owner: operations, with
  infrastructure on the guard/binding implementation. Until then, an operator removing such a gate
  hand-reverts its paths rather than reverting a commit, and devloop outputs should stop promising a
  gate-only revert they cannot deliver.

> **DECIDED by ADR-0035 (Accepted 2026-08-10) — do not re-litigate.** Every entry below was resolved in the ADR-0035 debate. The ADR owns the **decision, the grouping and the ordering**; these entries remain the **detail bodies** the ADR points at as single source. Read `docs/decisions/adr-0035-story-runner.md` §Follow-on work before planning any of them.
>
> **Hard constraint from the ADR**: §12 (the `run-story.sh` seams + test suite) is a **strict predecessor of everything in Group 1**. Landing a gate change, the suppression gate, or model tiering before it means using the live run as the test.
>
> **Group 3 hazard**: a fresh `claude -p` live-reads `/devloop`'s SKILL.md per task, so a broken edit there breaks the rest of an in-flight run. Group 3 items run last, and the preflight substrate probe must be re-run after any of them.
>
> The original batching note below is superseded: the ADR replaces "one skill-update pass" with three ordered groups, because the items differ in *hazard*, not just in size.

_(Superseded 2026-08-06 note, kept for provenance: "Batch these into one skill-update pass after Run #1 closes — user decision: hold, expect a decent number.")_

- [ ] **[ADR-0035 §13 · Group 2, runs immediately after Group 1]** **/user-story: acceptance-tests-first**: (1) acceptance tests become an early story task, committed RED with expected-fail annotations (Playwright `test.fail()` keeps the suite green-runnable all story); (2) `passes_after` mapping returns to the task manifest — the runner un-marks and runs the mapped tests when their task completes, so a still-red acceptance test is attributed to THAT task immediately; (3) ban "manual verification note" as acceptance criterion for machine-assertable behavior (the task #63 hole: passed all gates with the feature half-working; only task #60's late E2E assertion caught the AC→MC→UI break, forcing the backtrack/stash/staged-run dance).
- [x] **CLOSED 2026-08-17 — [ADR-0035 · Group 2] /user-story: emit the dt-story manifest at planning time.** Landed in story task 4 (R-8), devloop `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-08-17-manifest-single-home/`. Emission goes through `dt-story add-task --prompt-file` (Step 10.4), not hand-written YAML: `Manifest::to_block_body` refuses to write a manifest containing a markdown fence, so a prompt carrying a fenced example cannot silently truncate the block — a guarantee a prose rule could not provide. `--deps` was added to `add-task` for this. Step 10.5 is a blocking gate: `validate` (which now also rejects a zero-task manifest — a skeleton otherwise *asserts* the story is complete) plus an id-set **equality** check via `list-tasks`. Task ids are read back from stdout, never predicted: `add-task` assigns `max + 1` and the tag short-circuit does not advance it, so a predicted dep number points at the wrong task **and still validates**.
- [ ] **[ADR-0035 · Group 2]** **/close-story: verify Phase 4's PR synthesis scales to 60+-task stories** (it enumerates every devloop into the PR body).

  *The manifest-reading half of this entry CLOSED 2026-08-17* in story task 4 (R-8), devloop `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-08-17-manifest-single-home/`. Phase 1 now reads `dt-story list-tasks`; the §Devloop Tracking table is retired from the template and the skills.

  **Its prescription was WRONG and is corrected here rather than closed alongside a bug.** This entry said "Completeness = `dt-story next` exit 3" — and `next` is **not read-only**: it mutates on exactly one path, reopening an escalated task and rewriting the file, and that path is reachable *precisely when the completeness check would fail*. So the probe would be safe only when it passes and destructive when it fails. Ironically the mechanism was already documented five lines below, at the escalated-story entry, reproduced end-to-end against a fixture. The read-only verb is `list-tasks`, and completeness asserts `status == "completed"` **positively** (absence-of-`pending` silently passes an escalated task). ADR-0035 §4:92 carried the same error and is corrected in the same commit.

  **Pattern worth keeping**: `list-tasks` did not merely add a capability, it **expired a documented prescription** in two places. When a change supersedes a mechanism, `grep -rn` the docs for the old one — but classify each hit rather than rewriting it, because `next` is *correct* where it selects work to run (`run-story.sh`'s escalation-retry lane) and wrong only where it asks whether anything is outstanding.
- [ ] **[ADR-0035 · Group 2 · spun out of story task 4, owner `client`]** **`2026-05-02-browser-client-join.md`: project §Devloop Tracking down to its non-duplicated columns.** Story task 4 (R-8) retired that table everywhere else but deliberately left this file's alone, deleting only its `branch:` line. **Not deferred for effort — blocked on ownership**: the edit is not value-neutral (it adjudicates between two disagreeing records), `client` owns the file, and `client` was not a reviewer on that devloop, so ADR-0024 §6.3 could not be satisfied at any tier.

  **The residual, stated plainly**: that file now holds a second home for the slug mapping with **nothing in-file marking it superseded**. A frozen-record banner was considered and dropped — an in-file banner is read by whoever already opened the file, whereas ADR-0035 §4's new discriminator is read at the moment someone is *deciding* whether to delete a tracking table, which is when the mistake is actually made.

  **Do NOT simply delete the section, and do not backfill slugs into the manifest.** Measured 2026-08-17: 65 manifest tasks of which only 14 carry `prompt`/`specialist`/`env_tests` and 8 carry `deps` — **51 are bare `id` + `status` stubs**. Here the table is the **superset** and the manifest the **skeleton**, so deletion is destruction. The table also has 60 `/devloop` rows to the manifest's 65 (the runner-appended `audit-remediation-ts` task has no row), one row with an empty Devloop Output cell, and a status tally that disagrees with the manifest's.

  **Recipe (dry-reviewer's, recorded so it is a decision already made rather than a rediscovery)**: project the table to `| # | Devloop Output |`, dropping the four columns that duplicate the manifest (invocation, specialist, deps, status), and rename the heading to `## Devloop Output Index`. That resolves both known contradictions **by deletion rather than winner-picking** — the duplicate 18/19/20 rows and row 53's `Not implemented`, which is **not drift**: task 53's own prompt records the feature as deliberately not built and names this table as the home for that fact. Keep it factual and pointer-shaped.

- [ ] **[ADR-0035 · Group 2]** **/close-story + /devloop: stale `Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 (1M context)` commit trailers** (close-story SKILL.md:192, devloop SKILL.md:485) — should reflect the actual executing model, not a hardcoded name two generations old.
- [ ] **[Workflow sequencing trap · owner `operations`]** **Editing a devloop's `main.md` AFTER its own commit lands reds `validate-cross-boundary-scope` at Layer 3.** State the constraint positively: **devloop-record edits must be committed in the same operation that makes them.**

  **Mechanism, measured 2026-08-17** (devloop `2026-08-17-manifest-single-home`, found by running the guard rather than by reading the diff): a modified `main.md` marks that devloop **active**, and the guard then requires every path in its §Cross-Boundary Classification table to appear in the **working** diff. Once the devloop's commit has landed those files are in the commit, not the working tree, so the requirement is unsatisfiable by construction.

  ```
  clean tree            STATUS=OK   cross-boundary-scope-no-drift
  main.md edited only   STATUS=FAIL cross-boundary-scope-drift-planned-untouched-21
    VIOLATION: … [scope_drift_planned_untouched] "crates/dt-story/src/manifest.rs"   (+20 more)
  ```

  **Nothing here is broken.** The table is correct, the commit is correct, and the guard is correct — the failure is reachable only by the *combination*, i.e. by touching `main.md` after its own commit. That is why it needs recording rather than fixing: the next person to hit it will read it as their edit being wrong, not as a constraint on **when** the edit may be made, and will "fix" it by weakening the table or the guard.

  **Named victim, which is why this is a live trap and not a curiosity**: `/close-story` Phase 3 stages `docs/devloop-outputs/`, and the story-close gate is a full `layer-all.sh`. So **any** post-hoc correction to a devloop record — the exact thing a retrospective produces — reds Layer 3 unless the edit and its commit are adjacent. This bit during the recording of a retrospective entry, which is the likeliest way to meet it.

  **Options if it recurs often enough to be worth code**: (a) have the guard treat a `main.md` whose classification paths are satisfied by `HEAD`'s diff as in-scope, not just the working diff — closest to correct, and the scoping needs care so it cannot be used to bypass the check on a live devloop; (b) document-only, as here. Not fixed in the devloop that found it: the guard is not in that task's classification table, and changing a cross-boundary guard to unblock one's own record edit is the shape that should never be convenient.

- [ ] **[Review protocol · owner `code-reviewer`]** **Promote the "which call paths reach this control" review question to a shared surface** — `.claude/skills/devloop/review-protocol.md` or `docs/specialist-knowledge/code-reviewer/INDEX.md`. It currently lives only in `docs/devloop-outputs/2026-08-17-manifest-single-home/main.md`, which is a devloop narrative: read once, at Gate 3, by people who already know the lesson. Deliberately not written into a shared surface by that devloop — scope growth into the review protocol should be a decision, not a tail-end addition to a task already carrying a schema change, a CLI change, two skills and an ADR correction.

  **The rule, four clauses, capped at four.** Each is a question with a mechanically checkable answer, on the model of the referent-durability rule below (what makes that one work is not coverage but that someone can apply it in ten seconds): **(1) What exactly does this control compare — and is that the property that breaks?** **(2) Which call paths reach it?** **(3) Does it run before or after the transformation that destroys the evidence it needs?** **(4) Does the prose explaining this control still describe it?**

  **A fifth incident should sharpen an existing clause before it earns a new one** — one clause per incident is how a checklist becomes a litany nobody runs, which is itself a control that stopped being reached. Clause 4 has a construction-rule remedy the others lack: phrase rules as claims a test can assert, so decoupling reds instead of rotting.

  **Caught at FIVE layers, which is the argument for promoting it.** The rule is not fitted to one devloop. It found defects in a **production control** (a per-field validator structurally unreachable; a detector placed on the one path both gates bypass; an id-set check comparing ids where the loss was inside a prompt), in a **test fixture** (`run-story.test.sh`'s `claude` stub committed only `work.txt` and never its output dir, so four slug cases all silently exercised one path — a coverage metric would have reported four cases and four passes), and in a **verification fixture** (a scratch-repo check of the `:(glob)` pathspec whose first construction compared post-`cut` output instead of the pathspec's own match set, and returned a confident wrong answer).

  **The verification-fixture instance was the implementer's own, and self-caught.** The first fixture placed a nested `main.md` under a directory that also had a top-level one, so `cut -d/ -f3 | sort -u` collapsed the difference and both pathspec forms printed identically. Stopping there would have recorded "the downstream `cut` mitigates it, so the rationale does not hold" and left a comment in `run-story.sh` justifying a live control by a rationale just wrongly disproved. **That is clause 1 landing on the person applying it** — an entry in which every instance is someone else's is a rule about other people.
